It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Like even if it’s just sitting wrong or I washed them and they shrank a little, my brain goes straight to, “That’s it, I ruined everything.”\n\nAnd the scale too, if it goes up even a little. I know you asked about clothes, sorry. But it’s the same feeling. I panic, like by the end of the day I’m going to be fat or everyone’s going to see it. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number or maybe my pants shrunk in the dryer—working around all that laundry, you’d think I’d know better—but if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nOr if my waistband feels tight under my work apron, it’s like my whole brain goes, “That’s it, you ruined everything.” Like by the end of the day I’m gonna be fat, and everyone’s gonna notice. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? the only way I can calm down.\n- key: S045\n  name: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\n  description: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.\n  manifestation: If the scale goes up even a little, or my waistband feels tight under my work apron, I panic like I have ruined everything and I will be fat by the end of the day.\n- key: S046\n  name: catch my reflection in the glass and my stomach or hips look big to me.\n- key: S047\n  name: Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging\n  description: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).\n  manifestation: On days after I eat too much, I try to make up for it by not eating until late or walking and exercising even when my legs hurt from standing at the cleaners all day.\n- key: S049\n  name: Eating_Large_Amount\n  description: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n  manifestation: When it happens, I can go through a full bag of chips, pastries from the corner store, leftovers, and sweet drinks in one sitting before I even really notice how much is gone.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for food even while I am telling myself to stop, like I am watching myself do it and cannot make myself quit.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For months now, most weeks have at least a couple episodes where I eat way more than I meant to and then try to undo it by throwing up, fasting, or exercising it off.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My days get arranged around hiding what happened, and I have skipped after-work meals and rushed through errands or work tasks because I was preoccupied with how I was going to make up for eating.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 6 months\n laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Like even if it’s just sitting wrong or I washed them and they shrank a little, my brain goes straight to, “That’s it, I ruined everything.”\n\nAnd the scale too, if it goes up even a little. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Compared to other women my height? I mean… I don’t look at someone and think, “Okay, we’re the same height, let me compare.” I notice clothes and shapes and things, but it gets all mixed up in my head.\n\nLike at the laundry, I’ll see the same size pants come through and they look completely different depending on the person, or even how they’re folded, and then I start thinking about how mine fit, and… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I can’t say I think I’m underweight. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped getting your period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped completely. I mean, I’m pretty sure I still get it… I just don’t keep track very well.\n\nSometimes I’ll be trying to remember if something happened two weeks ago or a month ago, and then I think about the pile of shirts that came in that day at work, or whether it was before the weekend rush, and it all kind of runs together. But stopped altogether? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… three months is kind of hard for me to picture, because I don’t mark it down anywhere. I know that sounds bad, but I just sort of notice it when it happens and then move on.\n\nLike, I’ll remember needing to deal with it around a workday, but then I mix up whether that was last month or before that, because the weeks at the laundry feel the same sometimes. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep making myself throw up after I eat. Sometimes it’s at work, like in the bathroom there, which is awful because I’m always worried someone’s gonna hear, and the machines are loud but not always loud enough, you know? And sometimes it’s right when I get home.\n\nIt’s just… it started feeling like the only way I can calm down after eating, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number or maybe my pants shrunk in the dryer—working around all that laundry, you’d think I’d know better—but if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nOr if my waistband feels tight under my work apron, it’s like my whole brain goes, “That’s it, you ruined everything.” Like by the end of the day I’m gonna be fat, and everyone’s gonna notice. It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. I know you asked about clothes, sorry. But it’s the same feeling. I panic, like by the end of the day I’m going to be fat or everyone’s going to see it. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. But stopped altogether? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… three months is kind of hard for me to picture, because I don’t mark it down anywhere. I know that sounds bad, but I just sort of notice it when it happens and then move on.\n\nLike, I’ll remember needing to deal with it around a workday, but then I mix up whether that was last month or before that, because the weeks at the laundry feel the same sometimes. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I mean… irregular like, skipping around? I’m not really sure. I don’t keep a calendar for it or anything, so I wouldn’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nSometimes I think it comes at a different time, but then I’m trying to remember if it was before a busy week at work or after, and honestly those days all blend together. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep making myself throw up after I eat. Sometimes it’s at work, like in the bathroom there, which is awful because I’m always worried someone’s gonna hear, and the machines are loud but not always loud enough, you know? And sometimes it’s right when I get home.\n\nIt’s just… it started feeling like the only way I can calm down after eating, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number or maybe my pants shrunk in the dryer—working around all that laundry, you’d think I’d know better—but if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nOr if my waistband feels tight under my work apron, it’s like my whole brain goes, “That’s it, you ruined everything.” Like by the end of the day I’m gonna be fat, and everyone’s gonna notice. It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Like even if it’s just sitting wrong or I washed them and they shrank a little, my brain goes straight to, “That’s it, I ruined everything.”\n\nAnd the scale too, if it goes up even a little. I know you asked about clothes, sorry. But it’s the same feeling. I panic, like by the end of the day I’m going to be fat or everyone’s going to see it. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Compared to other women my height? I mean… I don’t look at someone and think, “Okay, we’re the same height, let me compare.” I notice clothes and shapes and things, but it gets all mixed up in my head.\n\nLike at the laundry, I’ll see the same size pants come through and they look completely different depending on the person, or even how they’re folded, and then I start thinking about how mine fit, and… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I can’t say I think I’m underweight. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t really have a clean “before and after” number in my head that I trust. Sometimes I think things fit different, but then I start wondering if it’s the dryer, or the fabric, or just me noticing too much.\n\nLike at work, some clothes come in looking totally stretched out and then after pressing they look different, so I get stuck thinking, “Well, maybe bodies are like that too,” which I know doesn’t really answer you.\n\nSo… significantly lower than it used to be? I don’t know. I can’t really say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get it, but I don’t really track it the way some people do, like with an app or anything. Sometimes I think, “Was that late?” but then I’m trying to remember what week it was, and my work schedule gets all mixed together because the days kind of blur at the laundry.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed anything I can say for sure. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped getting your period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped completely. individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n  manifestation: When it happens, I can go through a full bag of chips, pastries from the corner store, leftovers, and sweet drinks in one sitting before I even really notice how much is gone.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for food even while I am telling myself to stop, like I am watching myself do it and cannot make myself quit.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For months now, most weeks have at least a couple episodes where I eat way more than I meant to and then try to undo it by throwing up, fasting, or exercising it off.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My days get arranged around hiding what happened, and I have skipped after-work meals and rushed through errands or work tasks because I was preoccupied with how I was going to make up for eating.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about six months, since I started trying to remake my habits and image for the sustainable-textile showcase.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S048\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… three months is kind of hard for me to picture, because I don’t mark it down anywhere. I know that sounds bad, but I just sort of notice it when it happens and then move on.\n\nLike, I’ll remember needing to deal with it around a workday, but then I mix up whether that was last month or before that, because the weeks at the laundry feel the same sometimes. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I mean… irregular like, skipping around? I’m not really sure. I don’t keep a calendar for it or anything, so I wouldn’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nSometimes I think it comes at a different time, but then I’m trying to remember if it was before a busy week at work or after, and honestly those days all blend together. So… I haven’t noticed anything definite, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. Six months is a long time for me to keep straight without writing it down. I don’t think I went that whole time without one, but I can’t give you, like, dates or anything.\n\nSometimes I remember it because I had to deal with it before work, and then I start thinking about whether that was when we had all those uniforms come in at the laundry, and then I lose track of when it actually was. So… I think yes, but I’m not 100% sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had a period in the past year?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep making myself throw up after I eat. Sometimes it’s at work, like in the bathroom there, which is awful because I’m always worried someone’s gonna hear, and the machines are loud but not always loud enough, you know? And sometimes it’s right when I get home.\n\nIt’s just… it started feeling like the only way I can calm down after eating, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number or maybe my pants shrunk in the dryer—working around all that laundry, you’d think I’d know better—but if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nOr if my waistband feels tight under my work apron, it’s like my whole brain goes, “That’s it, you ruined everything.” Like by the end of the day I’m gonna be fat, and everyone’s gonna notice. It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Like even if it’s just sitting wrong or I washed them and they shrank a little, my brain goes straight to, “That’s it, I ruined everything.”\n\nAnd the scale too, if it goes up even a little. I know you asked about clothes, sorry. But it’s the same feeling. I panic, like by the end of the day I’m going to be fat or everyone’s going to see it. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Compared to other women my height? I mean… I don’t look at someone and think, “Okay, we’re the same height, let me compare.” I notice clothes and shapes and things, but it gets all mixed up in my head.\n\nLike at the laundry, I’ll see the same size pants come through and they look completely different depending on the person, or even how they’re folded, and then I start thinking about how mine fit, and… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I can’t say I think I’m underweight. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t really have a clean “before and after” number in my head that I trust. Sometimes I think things fit different, but then I start wondering if it’s the dryer, or the fabric, or just me noticing too much.\n\nLike at work, some clothes come in looking totally stretched out and then after pressing they look different, so I get stuck thinking, “Well, maybe bodies are like that too,” which I know doesn’t really answer you.\n\nSo… significantly lower than it used to be? I don’t know. I can’t really say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get it, but I don’t really track it the way some people do, like with an app or anything. Sometimes I think, “Was that late?” but then I’m trying to remember what week it was, and my work schedule gets all mixed together because the days kind of blur at the laundry.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed anything I can say for sure. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped getting your period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped completely. I mean, I’m pretty sure I still get it… I just don’t keep track very well.\n\nSometimes I’ll be trying to remember if something happened two weeks ago or a month ago, and then I think about the pile of shirts that came in that day at work, or whether it was before the weekend rush, and it all kind of runs together. But stopped altogether? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I mean… irregular like, skipping around? I don’t keep a calendar for it or anything, so I wouldn’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nSometimes I think it comes at a different time, but then I’m trying to remember if it was before a busy week at work or after, and honestly those days all blend together.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep making myself throw up after I eat. Sometimes it’s at work, like in the bathroom there, which is awful because I’m always worried someone’s gonna hear, and the machines are loud but not always loud enough, you know? And sometimes it’s right when I get home.\n\nIt’s just… it started feeling like the only way I can calm down after eating, and I hate that.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I am. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number or maybe my pants shrunk in the dryer—working around all that laundry, you’d think I’d know better—but if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nOr if my waistband feels tight under my work apron, it’s like my whole brain goes, “That’s it, you ruined everything.” Like by the end of the day I’m gonna be fat, and everyone’s gonna notice. It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Like even if it’s just sitting wrong or I washed them and they shrank a little, my brain goes straight to, “That’s it, I ruined everything.”\n\nAnd the scale too, if it goes up even a little. I know you asked about clothes, sorry. But it’s the same feeling. I panic, like by the end of the day I’m going to be fat or everyone’s going to see it. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Compared to other women my height? I mean… I don’t look at someone and think, “Okay, we’re the same height, let me compare.” I notice clothes and shapes and things, but it gets all mixed up in my head.\n\nLike at the laundry, I’ll see the same size pants come through and they look completely different depending on the person, or even how they’re folded, and then I start thinking about how mine fit, and… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I can’t say I think I’m underweight. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t really have a clean “before and after” number in my head that I trust. Sometimes I think things fit different, but then I start wondering if it’s the dryer, or the fabric, or just me noticing too much.\n\nLike at work, some clothes come in looking totally stretched out and then after pressing they look different, so I get stuck thinking, “Well, maybe bodies are like that too,” which I know doesn’t really answer you.\n\nSo… significantly lower than it used to be? I don’t know. I can’t really say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get it, but I don’t really track it the way some people do, like with an app or anything. Sometimes I think, “Was that late?” but then I’m trying to remember what week it was, and my work schedule gets all mixed together because the days kind of blur at the laundry.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed anything I can say for sure. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped getting your period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped completely. I mean, I’m pretty sure I still get it… I just don’t keep track very well.\n\nSometimes I’ll be trying to remember if something happened two weeks ago or a month ago, and then I think about the pile of shirts that came in that day at work, or whether it was before the weekend rush, and it all kind of runs together. But stopped altogether? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… three months is kind of hard for me to picture, because I don’t mark it down anywhere. I know that sounds bad, but I just sort of notice it when it happens and then move on.\n\nLike, I’ll remember needing to deal with it around a workday, but then I mix up whether that was last month or before that, because the weeks at the laundry feel the same sometimes. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I mean… irregular like, skipping around? I’m not really sure. I don’t keep a calendar for it or anything, so I wouldn’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nSometimes I think it comes at a different time, but then I’m trying to remember if it was before a busy week at work or after, and honestly those days all blend together. So… I haven’t noticed anything definite, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. Six months is a long time for me to keep straight without writing it down. I don’t think I went that whole time without one, but I can’t give you, like, dates or anything.\n\nSometimes I remember it because I had to deal with it before work, and then I start thinking about whether that was when we had all those uniforms come in at the laundry, and then I lose track of when it actually was. So… I think yes, but I’m not 100% sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past year?\n\nPatient: Over the past year? I mean… I probably have, yeah, but I don’t keep track of it in a way where I could swear to dates. A year is hard for me to think through, because I start trying to remember what was happening at work, like busy weeks at the laundry, and then I mix everything up.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s been a whole year without one, but I can’t tell you exactly when.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past year?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already said it, but… I really don’t know how to pin it down. Like, I don’t think it’s been a whole year without one, but then when I try to remember, I start matching it to stuff at work or what was going on that week, and then it all gets mixed up.\n\nSo I’d say probably yes, but I can’t give you a clear date or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just a number or maybe my pants shrunk in the dryer—working around all that laundry, you’d think I’d know better—but if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic.\n\nOr if my waistband feels tight under my work apron, it’s like my whole brain goes, “That’s it, you ruined everything.” Like by the end of the day I’m gonna be fat, and everyone’s gonna notice. It sounds dramatic saying it out loud, but in the moment it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or type of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Like if I feel like I ate too much the day before, I’ll tell myself I’m not eating until late, like I have to “make up for it.” And then at work I’m on my feet all day already, you know, at the cleaners, running back and forth with clothes and bags and people complaining about stains like I personally invented them… but I’ll still make myself walk more after, or exercise, even when my legs are hurting.\n\nIt’s not really normal, I guess. It feels like punishment, kind of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think it’s been, like, a huge amount? My weight kind of moves around, or I think it does, but then sometimes I don’t trust the scale because at home the floor’s uneven—my cousin told me that can mess it up, and then I started moving it around the bathroom like that would make me feel better.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d call it significant. I haven’t noticed some big drop, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight?\n\nPatient: Underweight? I don’t really know. I mean, I look in the mirror and it changes depending on the day, or the lighting, honestly. The mirrors at work are terrible too—there’s one near the back where we hang coats and it makes everyone look stretched out, I swear.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say that. I don’t know if I’m underweight. I mostly just know I don’t feel okay with what I see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know what “healthy” is supposed to feel like, honestly. Like, I can be having a perfectly normal day at the shop—getting things done, helping customers, not messing anything up—and then I catch my reflection in the front glass or one of those shiny machine doors, and if my stomach or my hips look big to me, it’s like… there goes the whole day.\n\nI’ll feel like I failed at something, even if nothing actually happened. So I don’t know if my weight is healthy for my height. I just know it doesn’t feel okay in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a weight that is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “lower than normal” sounds like there’s a chart somewhere, right? But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Compared to other women my height? I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t really have a clean “before and after” number in my head that I trust. Sometimes I think things fit different, but then I start wondering if it’s the dryer, or the fabric, or just me noticing too much.\n\nLike at work, some clothes come in looking totally stretched out and then after pressing they look different, so I get stuck thinking, “Well, maybe bodies are like that too,” which I know doesn’t really answer you.\n\nSo… significantly lower than it used to be? And I don’t really… I don’t keep track like that in a way I trust.\n\nAt work, there’s this one customer who always comments on people’s clothes fitting different after alterations—well, not alterations, we don’t do that, but like when things get cleaned and pressed—and sometimes I think, if strangers can notice things like that, maybe I’d notice if I looked too small. But then I don’t know what other people see.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can say I’m lower than normal for my height. I just don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I notice it mostly with my work pants, because I’ve got the apron tied over them all day and if the waistband feels tight under it, I just— I can’t stop thinking about it. Like even if it’s just sitting wrong or I washed them and they shrank a little, my brain goes straight to, “That’s it, I ruined everything.”\n\nAnd the scale too, if it goes up even a little. I know you asked about clothes, sorry. But it’s the same feeling. I panic, like by the end of the day I’m going to be fat or everyone’s going to see it. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Compared to other women my height? I mean… I don’t look at someone and think, “Okay, we’re the same height, let me compare.” I notice clothes and shapes and things, but it gets all mixed up in my head.\n\nLike at the laundry, I’ll see the same size pants come through and they look completely different depending on the person, or even how they’re folded, and then I start thinking about how mine fit, and… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I can’t say I think I’m underweight. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t really have a clean “before and after” number in my head that I trust. Sometimes I think things fit different, but then I start wondering if it’s the dryer, or the fabric, or just me noticing too much.\n\nLike at work, some clothes come in looking totally stretched out and then after pressing they look different, so I get stuck thinking, “Well, maybe bodies are like that too,” which I know doesn’t really answer you.\n\nSo… significantly lower than it used to be? I don’t know. I can’t really say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get it, but I don’t really track it the way some people do, like with an app or anything. Sometimes I think, “Was that late?” but then I’m trying to remember what week it was, and my work schedule gets all mixed together because the days kind of blur at the laundry.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed anything I can say for sure. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped getting your period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped completely. I mean, I’m pretty sure I still get it… I just don’t keep track very well.\n\nSometimes I’ll be trying to remember if something happened two weeks ago or a month ago, and then I think about the pile of shirts that came in that day at work, or whether it was before the weekend rush, and it all kind of runs together. But stopped altogether? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… three months is kind of hard for me to picture, because I don’t mark it down anywhere. I know that sounds bad, but I just sort of notice it when it happens and then move on.\n\nLike, I’ll remember needing to deal with it around a workday, but then I mix up whether that was last month or before that, because the weeks at the laundry feel the same sometimes. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I mean… irregular like, skipping around? I’m not really sure. I don’t keep a calendar for it or anything, so I wouldn’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nSometimes I think it comes at a different time, but then I’m trying to remember if it was before a busy week at work or after, and honestly those days all blend together. So… I haven’t noticed anything definite, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. Six months is a long time for me to keep straight without writing it down. I don’t think I went that whole time without one, but I can’t give you, like, dates or anything.\n\nSometimes I remember it because I had to deal with it before work, and then I start thinking about whether that was when we had all those uniforms come in at the laundry, and then I lose track of when it actually was. So… I think yes, but I’m not 100% sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past year?\n\nPatient: Over the past year? I mean… I probably have, yeah, but I don’t keep track of it in a way where I could swear to dates. A year is hard for me to think through, because I start trying to remember what was happening at work, like busy weeks at the laundry, and then I mix everything up.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s been a whole year without one, but I can’t tell you exactly when.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past year?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already said it, but… I really don’t know how to pin it down. Like, I don’t think it’s been a whole year without one, but then when I try to remember, I start matching it to stuff at work or what was going on that week, and then it all gets mixed up.\n\nSo I’d say probably yes, but I can’t give you a clear date or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past 12 months?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re asking the same thing and I’m not trying to be difficult. Twelve months… I don’t think I’ve gone that whole time without one, but I can’t say it like a definite yes with a date.\n\nI remember having to deal with it at some point, but then I start thinking, was that around when work got backed up, or when we had all those sheets come in, and then I’m not sure if I’m putting the memory in the right year. Then I’m tugging at my shirt and checking the apron, and it messes with me the whole shift.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are underweight compared to other people of your same height?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Compared to other women my height? I mean… I don’t look at someone and think, “Okay, we’re the same height, let me compare.” I notice clothes and shapes and things, but it gets all mixed up in my head.\n\nLike at the laundry, I’ll see the same size pants come through and they look completely different depending on the person, or even how they’re folded, and then I start thinking about how mine fit, and… sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I can’t say I think I’m underweight. I don’t know what other people would say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is significantly lower than it used to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t really have a clean “before and after” number in my head that I trust. Sometimes I think things fit different, but then I start wondering if it’s the dryer, or the fabric, or just me noticing too much.\n\nLike at work, some clothes come in looking totally stretched out and then after pressing they look different, so I get stuck thinking, “Well, maybe bodies are like that too,” which I know doesn’t really answer you.\n\nSo… significantly lower than it used to be? I don’t know. I can’t really say that for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I get it, but I don’t really track it the way some people do, like with an app or anything. Sometimes I think, “Was that late?” but then I’m trying to remember what week it was, and my work schedule gets all mixed together because the days kind of blur at the laundry.\n\nSo… I haven’t noticed anything I can say for sure. I’d have to think about it more, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped getting your period?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped completely. I mean, I’m pretty sure I still get it… I just don’t keep track very well.\n\nSometimes I’ll be trying to remember if something happened two weeks ago or a month ago, and then I think about the pile of shirts that came in that day at work, or whether it was before the weekend rush, and it all kind of runs together. But stopped altogether? I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past three months?\n\nPatient: I think so? I mean… three months is kind of hard for me to picture, because I don’t mark it down anywhere. I know that sounds bad, but I just sort of notice it when it happens and then move on.\n\nLike, I’ll remember needing to deal with it around a workday, but then I mix up whether that was last month or before that, because the weeks at the laundry feel the same sometimes. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m totally sure, but I don’t think it’s been three whole months without one.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any irregularity in your menstrual cycle?\n\nPatient: I mean… irregular like, skipping around? I’m not really sure. I don’t keep a calendar for it or anything, so I wouldn’t want to say yes or no like I know for sure.\n\nSometimes I think it comes at a different time, but then I’m trying to remember if it was before a busy week at work or after, and honestly those days all blend together. So… I haven’t noticed anything definite, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. Six months is a long time for me to keep straight without writing it down. I don’t think I went that whole time without one, but I can’t give you, like, dates or anything.\n\nSometimes I remember it because I had to deal with it before work, and then I start thinking about whether that was when we had all those uniforms come in at the laundry, and then I lose track of when it actually was. So… I think yes, but I’m not 100% sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past year?\n\nPatient: Over the past year? I mean… I probably have, yeah, but I don’t keep track of it in a way where I could swear to dates. A year is hard for me to think through, because I start trying to remember what was happening at work, like busy weeks at the laundry, and then I mix everything up.\n\nSo… I don’t think it’s been a whole year without one, but I can’t tell you exactly when.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past year?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of already said it, but… I really don’t know how to pin it down. Like, I don’t think it’s been a whole year without one, but then when I try to remember, I start matching it to stuff at work or what was going on that week, and then it all gets mixed up.\n\nSo I’d say probably yes, but I can’t give you a clear date or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period in the past 12 months?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re asking the same thing and I’m not trying to be difficult. Twelve months… I don’t think I’ve gone that whole time without one, but I can’t say it like a definite yes with a date.\n\nI remember having to deal with it at some point, but then I start thinking, was that around when work got backed up, or when we had all those sheets come in, and then I’m not sure if I’m putting the memory in the right year. So… probably, but I don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods at all in the past year?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least once, but I can’t be certain enough to say it cleanly. I don’t mark it down or anything. Sometimes I remember because of what I was wearing at work, or like having to deal with it during a shift, but then I start thinking about which month that was and whether it was actually this past year, and my brain just kind of… goes sideways.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it’s been zero periods in the whole year. I just can’t give you a definite count or date.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods at all in the past two years?\n\nPatient: Two years… I mean, I really think I’ve had some in that time, yeah. I don’t think it’s been two whole years with nothing.\n\nBut I’m not great with dates. I don’t keep track, and then I start trying to remember it by what was happening at work or whether I was doing certain loads that day, and it all just gets mixed up. So I can’t tell you how many or when exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
